# Azure SQL — complete SKU table

Every Azure SQL Database and Azure SQL Managed Instance SKU that Microsoft currently documents: **293 SKUs**. Generated 2026-10-05 by the `CurrentAzureSQLSKUInfo` skill, direct from Microsoft Learn article sources.

Column notes:

- **Release date** — the GA date of the release that made the SKU orderable, or the preview date for SKUs still in preview. Microsoft publishes release dates per SKU *family*, not per individual size, so sizes added later than their family carry their own date.
- **Confidence** — how firmly the date is sourced. `high` = a dated Microsoft announcement names this exact change; `medium` = a Microsoft page dates it to a month; `low` = reconstructed from context, treat as approximate.
- **Lifecycle status** — read from the current Microsoft documentation.
- **When to recommend** — paraphrased from the Microsoft pages linked in the last column. No third-party or inferred advice.
- **Memory** — transcribed from the published `Memory (GB)` rows for Azure SQL Database; derived from the documented per-vCore ratio and cap for Managed Instance, whose docs publish ratios rather than a per-size table.

| # | SKU | Deployment | Service tier | Hardware | Size | Release date | Confidence | Lifecycle status | SKU data source | Release date source | When to recommend this SKU | Recommendation source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `Basic` | Single database | Basic | n/a (DTU model) | 5 DTU | 2014-09 | medium | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-single-databases) | [announcement](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/new-azure-sql-database-service-tiers-generally-available-in-september-with-reduced-pricing-and-enhanced-sla/) | 1. Purchasing model: pick DTU only if you want simple, preconfigured bundles of compute, storage, and I/O. Microsoft marks the vCore model as recommended, and Azure Hybrid Benefit, the serverless compute tier, and the Hyperscale service tier are all unavailable in the DTU model.<br>2. Use for development, testing, and other infrequently accessed workloads that are less sensitive to performance variability - Basic data files sit on HDD-based Standard page blob storage.<br>3. Only use when the database fits in 2 GB; that is the hard storage ceiling for the Basic tier.<br>4. Do not use for resource-intensive or CPU-intensive work: Basic provides less than one vCore, and Microsoft recommends S3 or greater instead.<br>5. Do not use if you need more than 7 days of point-in-time restore; Basic caps backup retention at 1-7 days while Standard and Premium go to 35 days.<br>6. Avoid if you need columnstore indexing or In-Memory OLTP - neither is available in Basic. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-dtu)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-single-databases) |
| 2 | `S0` | Single database | Standard | n/a (DTU model) | 10 DTU | 2014-09 | medium | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-single-databases) | [announcement](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/new-azure-sql-database-service-tiers-generally-available-in-september-with-reduced-pricing-and-enhanced-sla/) | 1. Purchasing model: pick DTU only if you want simple, preconfigured bundles of compute, storage, and I/O. Microsoft marks the vCore model as recommended, and Azure Hybrid Benefit, the serverless compute tier, and the Hyperscale service tier are all unavailable in the DTU model.<br>2. Use for development, test, and light production workloads that fit within the 250 GB storage limit of S0-S2.<br>3. S0 and S1 store data files on HDD-based Standard storage, so pick them only where performance variability is acceptable.<br>4. Do not use for CPU-intensive workloads: S0, S1, and S2 each provide less than one vCore, and Microsoft recommends S3 or greater.<br>5. Do not use as the target of a database restore - restore operations are resource-intensive and often require S3 or greater. You can scale back down afterwards. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-dtu)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-single-databases) |

| 5 | `S3` | Single database | Standard | n/a (DTU model) | 100 DTU | 2014-09 | medium | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-single-databases) | [announcement](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/new-azure-sql-database-service-tiers-generally-available-in-september-with-reduced-pricing-and-enhanced-sla/) | 1. Microsoft marks the vCore model as recommended, and Azure Hybrid Benefit, the serverless compute tier, and the Hyperscale service tier are all unavailable in the DTU model.<br>2. Use for development and production workloads that need up to 1 TB of storage and up to 3000 DTU of blended compute.<br>3. Choose S3 or greater whenever the workload is CPU-intensive, since S3 is the first Standard objective with a full vCore or more.<br>4. Choose S3 or greater when the database is the target of a restore operation.<br>5. S3 and above are the lowest Standard objectives that support columnstore indexing.<br>6. Move up to Premium instead if you need In-Memory OLTP or approximately 2 ms IO latency - Standard delivers about 5 ms read and 10 ms write. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-dtu)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-single-databases) |

| 11 | `P1` | Single database | Premium | n/a (DTU model) | 125 DTU | 2014-09 | medium | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-single-databases) | [announcement](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/new-azure-sql-database-service-tiers-generally-available-in-september-with-reduced-pricing-and-enhanced-sla/) | 1. Microsoft marks the vCore model as recommended, and Azure Hybrid Benefit, the serverless compute tier, and the Hyperscale service tier are all unavailable in the DTU model.<br>2. Use for IO-intensive production workloads: Premium delivers more than 25 IOPS per DTU versus 1-4 IOPS per DTU in Basic and Standard.<br>3. Use when the workload needs low IO latency - approximately 2 ms read and write, against about 5 ms read and 10 ms write in Basic and Standard.<br>4. Choose Premium when you need In-Memory OLTP, which is not available in the Basic or Standard tiers.<br>5. Choose P11 or P15 when the database needs more than 1 TB of storage, up to the 4 TB Premium maximum.<br>6. Check region support before choosing P11 or P15 above 1 TB: storage is capped at 1 TB in China East, China North, Germany Central, and Germany Northeast. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-dtu)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-single-databases) |

| 17 | `BasicPool_50` | Elastic pool | Basic | n/a (DTU model) | 50 eDTU | 2014-09 | medium | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-elastic-pools) | [announcement](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/new-azure-sql-database-service-tiers-generally-available-in-september-with-reduced-pricing-and-enhanced-sla/) | 1. Purchasing model: pick DTU only if you want simple, preconfigured bundles of compute, storage, and I/O. Microsoft marks the vCore model as recommended, and Azure Hybrid Benefit, the serverless compute tier, and the Hyperscale service tier are all unavailable in the DTU model.<br>2. Use when you have many databases with a low average resource utilization and relatively infrequent utilization spikes.<br>3. Use when per-database usage is unpredictable and you want a single, predictable budget for the group rather than provisioning each database separately.<br>4. Use when you want a guarantee that no single database can consume all the resources, while every database keeps a minimum reservation.<br>5. Move a database out of the pool and onto a single database if it shows consistently high utilization that impacts the others.<br>6. Match the pool tier to the databases: Basic pools cap at 5 eDTU and 2 GB per database, Standard pools at 3000 eDTU, Premium pools at 4000 eDTU and 100 databases per pool. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-dtu)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-elastic-pools)<br>[4](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/elastic-pool-overview) |

Move a database out of the pool and onto a single database if it shows consistently high utilization that impacts the others.<br>6. Match the pool tier to the databases: Basic pools cap at 5 eDTU and 2 GB per database, Standard pools at 3000 eDTU, Premium pools at 4000 eDTU and 100 databases per pool. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-dtu)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-dtu-elastic-pools)<br>[4](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/elastic-pool-overview) |

| 46 | `GP_S_Gen5_1` | Single database / Elastic pool | General Purpose — Serverless | Standard-series (Gen5) | 1 vCore | 2019-11 | medium | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-vcore-single-databases) | [announcement](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/serverless-tier-overview) | 1. Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Use for single databases with intermittent, unpredictable usage interspersed with periods of inactivity and low average compute utilization over time.<br>3. Use for databases you currently rescale frequently and would rather delegate compute rescaling to the service.<br>4. Use for new databases with no usage history, where compute sizing is hard or impossible to estimate before deployment.<br>5. Only choose it if the workload can tolerate warm-up delay after an idle period, and the application implements connection retry logic - auto-resume connectivity errors are predictable.<br>6. Do not choose it for regular, predictable usage with high average utilization; provisioned compute has a lower vCore unit price for those.<br>7. Do not choose it for several intermittent databases that could instead be consolidated into an elastic pool for better price-performance.<br>8. Availability constraint: serverless runs only on standard-series (Gen5), only in the vCore model, and is not offered in Business Critical. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/serverless-tier-overview)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-sql-database-vcore) |

Do not choose it for regular, predictable usage with high average utilization; provisioned compute has a lower vCore unit price for those.<br>7. Do not choose it for several intermittent databases that could instead be consolidated into an elastic pool for better price-performance.<br>8. Availability constraint: serverless runs only on standard-series (Gen5), only in the vCore model, and is not offered in Business Critical. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/serverless-tier-overview)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-sql-database-vcore) |

| 90 | `HS_PRMS_2` | Single database / Elastic pool | Hyperscale | Premium-series | 2 vCore / 10.4 GB | 2023-07 | high | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-vcore-single-databases) | [announcement](https://aka.ms/AAiq28n) | 1. Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Choose over standard-series when you want a guarantee of running on newer CPUs; standard-series gives no such guarantee and may be placed on older hardware.<br>3. Choose when a single Hyperscale database or pool needs more than the 80 vCores that standard-series tops out at - premium-series reaches 128.<br>4. Cost is not a reason to avoid it: there is no price difference between premium-series and standard-series at the same vCore count.<br>5. Check regional availability first - premium-series is not offered in every region, whereas standard-series is.<br>6. Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Microsoft's recommended and default service tier for all new and modernizing OLTP and HTAP workloads, not only large databases.<br>3. Use when the database may grow past the 4 TB ceiling of General Purpose and Business Critical - Hyperscale scales to 128 TB.<br>4. Use when you want to pay only for allocated data storage rather than a provisioned maximum, with a 10 GB minimum and no charge for log storage.<br>5. Use when you want to choose the number of high availability replicas from 0 to 4 to trade resiliency against cost.<br>6. Use when you need read scale-out through named replicas, fast scaling without waiting for data to copy, or fast restore of a large database.<br>7. Do not choose it if the workload requires In-Memory OLTP tables, which Hyperscale does not support.<br>8. Note that Azure Hybrid Benefit is not available for new Hyperscale databases as of December 2023. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-sql-database-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tier-hyperscale) |

Use when the database may grow past the 4 TB ceiling of General Purpose and Business Critical - Hyperscale scales to 128 TB.<br>4. Use when you want to pay only for allocated data storage rather than a provisioned maximum, with a 10 GB minimum and no charge for log storage.<br>5. Use when you want to choose the number of high availability replicas from 0 to 4 to trade resiliency against cost.<br>6. Use when you need read scale-out through named replicas, fast scaling without waiting for data to copy, or fast restore of a large database.<br>7. Do not choose it if the workload requires In-Memory OLTP tables, which Hyperscale does not support.<br>8. Note that Azure Hybrid Benefit is not available for new Hyperscale databases as of December 2023. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-sql-database-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tier-hyperscale) |

Choose when you need Always Encrypted with secure enclaves backed by hardware enclaves (Intel SGX) rather than Virtualization-based Security enclaves.<br>3. Confirm your subscription qualifies: DC-series requires a paid offer type such as Pay-As-You-Go or Enterprise Agreement.<br>5. Do not choose it if you need the serverless compute tier or zone redundancy - DC-series supports neither.<br>6. Check regional availability first; DC-series is offered only in selected regions, unlike standard-series.<br>7. Size within 2-40 vCores at 4.5 GB of memory per vCore, which is below the 5.1 GB per vCore of standard-series. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-sql-database-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-vcore-single-databases) |

| 134 | `GP_Gen5_2` | Single database / Elastic pool | General Purpose | Standard-series (Gen5) | 2 vCore / 10.4 GB | 2018-04 | low | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-vcore-single-databases) | [announcement](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/a-flexible-new-way-to-purchase-azure-sql-database/) | 1. Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Default choice for most generic Azure SQL Database workloads that need a fully managed engine with the standard SLA.<br>3. Use when storage latency between 5 ms and 10 ms is acceptable for the workload.<br>4. Use for budget-oriented workloads wanting balanced compute and storage, at roughly a third the price of Business Critical for the same vCore count.<br>5. Use when the database fits within 4 TB; move to Hyperscale beyond that.<br>6. Standard-series (Gen5) is available in every public region worldwide, so pick it when broad regional coverage matters.<br>7. Do not choose it if you need In-Memory OLTP tables or a free read-scale replica - both require Business Critical. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-sql-database-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-vcore-single-databases) |

Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Use when the workload needs consistently low IO latency, about 1-2 ms on average, from locally attached SSD storage.<br>3. Use when reporting, analytics, or other read-only queries can be offloaded to the free read-scale replica included in the tier.<br>4. Use when you need higher resiliency and faster recovery: three secondary replicas mean a secondary is promoted immediately on failure.<br>5. Use when the workload needs In-Memory OLTP, which General Purpose and Hyperscale do not offer.<br>6. Use when active geo-replication must guarantee an RPO of 5 seconds and an RTO of 30 seconds.<br>7. Budget for roughly 2.7 times the General Purpose price, which is what the three extra replicas cost. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-sql-database-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/resource-limits-vcore-single-databases) |

| 188 | `GP_Gen5 (2 vCores)` | Managed instance | General Purpose | Standard-series (Gen5) | 2 vCore / 10.2 GB | 2024-11 | high | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) | [announcement](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/doc-changes-updates-release-notes-whats-new-archive) | 1. Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Default service tier for most generic managed instance workloads that need a fully managed engine with the standard SLA.<br>3. Use when storage latency of 5-10 ms is acceptable for the workload.<br>4. Use when you need SQL Server instance-scoped surface area and near-100% engine compatibility rather than a single database.<br>5. Use when you can stop and start the instance during idle periods to save compute and licensing cost - a General Purpose-only capability.<br>6. Stay within the limits: 80 vCores, 16 TB of instance storage, and 100 user databases per instance.<br>7. Do not choose it if you need In-Memory OLTP or a read-only replica; those require Business Critical.<br>8. Baseline managed instance hardware - choose it when 5.1 GB of memory per vCore and a ceiling of 80 vCores and 408 GB are enough.<br>9. Choose it when regional coverage matters: standard-series is available in all public regions worldwide.<br>10. Use an instance pool if you want a 2-vCore instance; 2 vCores cannot be deployed as a standalone instance.<br>11. Move to premium-series if the workload needs more memory per vCore or more than 80 vCores. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-managed-instance-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) |

| 197 | `GP_Gen5 (4 vCores)` | Managed instance | Next-gen General Purpose | Standard-series (Gen5) | 4 vCore / 20.4 GB | 2025-11 | high | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) | [announcement](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/doc-changes-updates-release-notes-whats-new-archive) | 1. Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Choose when your business is budget-oriented but the performance metrics and limits of the classic General Purpose tier are insufficient - the baseline cost is the same.<br>3. Choose when you need more than 100 databases on one instance; Next-gen supports up to 500.<br>4. Choose when you need more than 16 TB of reserved storage; Next-gen supports up to 32 TB.<br>5. Choose when you need better storage latency, IOPS, and throughput - it uses Elastic SAN instead of page blobs, giving 3-5 ms latency against 5-10 ms.<br>6. Choose when you want to scale vCores, memory, storage, and IOPS independently, including flexible memory on premium-series hardware.<br>7. Budget for the extras: IOPS above the free quota of 3 per GB of reserved storage, and memory above the default allocation, are billed separately.<br>8. Note that In-Memory OLTP is still not supported, and there are still no read-only replicas.<br>9. Baseline managed instance hardware - choose it when 5.1 GB of memory per vCore and a ceiling of 80 vCores and 408 GB are enough.<br>10. Choose it when regional coverage matters: standard-series is available in all public regions worldwide.<br>11. Use an instance pool if you want a 2-vCore instance; 2 vCores cannot be deployed as a standalone instance.<br>12. Move to premium-series if the workload needs more memory per vCore or more than 80 vCores. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-managed-instance-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-next-gen-general-purpose-use)<br>[4](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) |

Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Use when the workload needs consistently low IO latency, about 1-2 ms, from locally attached SSD storage.<br>3. Use when reports, analytics, or read-only queries can be redirected to the free read-scale replica included in the tier.<br>4. Use when you need higher resiliency and faster recovery - one of three secondary replicas becomes the new primary immediately on failure.<br>5. Use when you need In-Memory OLTP, which neither General Purpose nor Next-gen General Purpose supports.<br>6. Use when advanced data corruption protection through automatic page repair matters.<br>7. Use when a failover group must guarantee an RPO of 5 seconds and an RTO of 30 seconds.<br>8. Configure 64 or more vCores if you need compute isolation - it is only supported at that size and above.<br>9. Plan capacity accordingly: one Business Critical vCore consumes four regional vCore units against your subscription quota, versus one for General Purpose.<br>10. Baseline managed instance hardware - choose it when 5.1 GB of memory per vCore and a ceiling of 80 vCores and 408 GB are enough.<br>11. Choose it when regional coverage matters: standard-series is available in all public regions worldwide.<br>12. Use an instance pool if you want a 2-vCore instance; 2 vCores cannot be deployed as a standalone instance.<br>13. Use when a failover group must guarantee an RPO of 5 seconds and an RTO of 30 seconds.<br>8. Configure 64 or more vCores if you need compute isolation - it is only supported at that size and above.<br>9. Plan capacity accordingly: one Business Critical vCore consumes four regional vCore units against your subscription quota, versus one for General Purpose.<br>10. Baseline managed instance hardware - choose it when 5.1 GB of memory per vCore and a ceiling of 80 vCores and 408 GB are enough.<br>11. Choose it when regional coverage matters: standard-series is available in all public regions worldwide.<br>12. Use an instance pool if you want a 2-vCore instance; 2 vCores cannot be deployed as a standalone instance.<br>13. Move to premium-series if the workload needs more memory per vCore or more than 80 vCores. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-managed-instance-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) |

Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Default service tier for most generic managed instance workloads that need a fully managed engine with the standard SLA.<br>3. Use when you need SQL Server instance-scoped surface area and near-100% engine compatibility rather than a single database.<br>5. Use when you can stop and start the instance during idle periods to save compute and licensing cost - a General Purpose-only capability.<br>6. Stay within the limits: 80 vCores, 16 TB of instance storage, and 100 user databases per instance.<br>7. Choose when the workload needs 7 GB of memory per vCore instead of 5.1 GB, up to 560 GB per instance.<br>10. Choose when you need more than 80 vCores - premium-series scales to 128 in Business Critical and Next-gen General Purpose.<br>11. Required if you want the flexible memory feature, which lets you raise memory without adding vCores.<br>12. Choose when Business Critical needs up to 16 TB of storage, available in major regions (5.5 TB elsewhere).<br>13. Regional coverage is broad: premium-series is available in all public regions. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-managed-instance-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) |

Choose when you want to scale vCores, memory, storage, and IOPS independently, including flexible memory on premium-series hardware.<br>7. Budget for the extras: IOPS above the free quota of 3 per GB of reserved storage, and memory above the default allocation, are billed separately.<br>8. Note that In-Memory OLTP is still not supported, and there are still no read-only replicas.<br>9. Choose when you want the newer Intel 8370C (Ice Lake) 2.8 GHz processors rather than the mixed Broadwell/Skylake/Cascade Lake pool of standard-series.<br>10. Required if you want the flexible memory feature, which lets you raise memory without adding vCores.<br>13. Choose when you need better storage latency, IOPS, and throughput - it uses Elastic SAN instead of page blobs, giving 3-5 ms latency against 5-10 ms.<br>6. Choose when you want to scale vCores, memory, storage, and IOPS independently, including flexible memory on premium-series hardware.<br>7. Budget for the extras: IOPS above the free quota of 3 per GB of reserved storage, and memory above the default allocation, are billed separately.<br>8. Note that In-Memory OLTP is still not supported, and there are still no read-only replicas.<br>9. Choose when you want the newer Intel 8370C (Ice Lake) 2.8 GHz processors rather than the mixed Broadwell/Skylake/Cascade Lake pool of standard-series.<br>10. Choose when the workload needs 7 GB of memory per vCore instead of 5.1 GB, up to 560 GB per instance.<br>11. Choose when you need more than 80 vCores - premium-series scales to 128 in Business Critical and Next-gen General Purpose.<br>12. Required if you want the flexible memory feature, which lets you raise memory without adding vCores.<br>13. Choose when Business Critical needs up to 16 TB of storage, available in major regions (5.5 TB elsewhere).<br>14. Regional coverage is broad: premium-series is available in all public regions. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-managed-instance-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-next-gen-general-purpose-use)<br>[4](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) |

Choose when Business Critical needs up to 16 TB of storage, available in major regions (5.5 TB elsewhere).<br>14. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Use when the workload needs consistently low IO latency, about 1-2 ms, from locally attached SSD storage.<br>3. Use when reports, analytics, or read-only queries can be redirected to the free read-scale replica included in the tier.<br>4. Use when you need higher resiliency and faster recovery - one of three secondary replicas becomes the new primary immediately on failure.<br>5. Use when you need In-Memory OLTP, which neither General Purpose nor Next-gen General Purpose supports.<br>6. Use when advanced data corruption protection through automatic page repair matters.<br>7. Use when a failover group must guarantee an RPO of 5 seconds and an RTO of 30 seconds.<br>8. Configure 64 or more vCores if you need compute isolation - it is only supported at that size and above.<br>9. Plan capacity accordingly: one Business Critical vCore consumes four regional vCore units against your subscription quota, versus one for General Purpose.<br>10. Choose when you want the newer Intel 8370C (Ice Lake) 2.8 GHz processors rather than the mixed Broadwell/Skylake/Cascade Lake pool of standard-series.<br>11. Required if you want the flexible memory feature, which lets you raise memory without adding vCores.<br>14. Choose when Business Critical needs up to 16 TB of storage, available in major regions (5.5 TB elsewhere).<br>15. Use when a failover group must guarantee an RPO of 5 seconds and an RTO of 30 seconds.<br>8. Configure 64 or more vCores if you need compute isolation - it is only supported at that size and above.<br>9. Plan capacity accordingly: one Business Critical vCore consumes four regional vCore units against your subscription quota, versus one for General Purpose.<br>10. Choose when you want the newer Intel 8370C (Ice Lake) 2.8 GHz processors rather than the mixed Broadwell/Skylake/Cascade Lake pool of standard-series.<br>11. Choose when the workload needs 7 GB of memory per vCore instead of 5.1 GB, up to 560 GB per instance.<br>12. Choose when you need more than 80 vCores - premium-series scales to 128 in Business Critical and Next-gen General Purpose.<br>13. Required if you want the flexible memory feature, which lets you raise memory without adding vCores.<br>14. Choose when Business Critical needs up to 16 TB of storage, available in major regions (5.5 TB elsewhere).<br>15. Regional coverage is broad: premium-series is available in all public regions. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-managed-instance-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) |

| 254 | `GP_G8IH (4 vCores)` | Managed instance | General Purpose | Premium-series memory optimized | 4 vCore / 54.4 GB | 2022-09 | medium | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) | [announcement](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/doc-changes-updates-release-notes-whats-new-archive) | 1. Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Default service tier for most generic managed instance workloads that need a fully managed engine with the standard SLA.<br>3. Use when storage latency of 5-10 ms is acceptable for the workload.<br>4. Use when you need SQL Server instance-scoped surface area and near-100% engine compatibility rather than a single database.<br>5. Use when you can stop and start the instance during idle periods to save compute and licensing cost - a General Purpose-only capability.<br>6. Stay within the limits: 80 vCores, 16 TB of instance storage, and 100 user databases per instance.<br>7. Do not choose it if you need In-Memory OLTP or a read-only replica; those require Business Critical.<br>8. Choose when the workload is memory-bound and needs 13.6 GB of memory per vCore, up to 870.4 GB per instance.<br>9. Choose when In-Memory OLTP needs the largest available allocation - memory optimized premium-series gives roughly double the premium-series limit.<br>10. Choose when Business Critical needs up to 16 TB of instance storage.<br>11. Check regional availability first - memory optimized premium-series is restricted to a subset of regions, unlike standard-series and premium-series.<br>12. Note the memory-to-vCore ratio holds only up to 64 vCores; above that, memory stays capped at 870.4 GB. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-managed-instance-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) |

Default service tier for most generic managed instance workloads that need a fully managed engine with the standard SLA.<br>3. Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Choose when your business is budget-oriented but the performance metrics and limits of the classic General Purpose tier are insufficient - the baseline cost is the same.<br>3. Choose when you need more than 100 databases on one instance; Next-gen supports up to 500.<br>4. Choose when you need more than 16 TB of reserved storage; Next-gen supports up to 32 TB.<br>5. Choose when you need better storage latency, IOPS, and throughput - it uses Elastic SAN instead of page blobs, giving 3-5 ms latency against 5-10 ms.<br>6. Choose when you want to scale vCores, memory, storage, and IOPS independently, including flexible memory on premium-series hardware.<br>7. Budget for the extras: IOPS above the free quota of 3 per GB of reserved storage, and memory above the default allocation, are billed separately.<br>8. Note that In-Memory OLTP is still not supported, and there are still no read-only replicas.<br>9. Choose when the workload is memory-bound and needs 13.6 GB of memory per vCore, up to 870.4 GB per instance.<br>10. Choose when In-Memory OLTP needs the largest available allocation - memory optimized premium-series gives roughly double the premium-series limit.<br>11. Choose when Business Critical needs up to 16 TB of instance storage.<br>12. Check regional availability first - memory optimized premium-series is restricted to a subset of regions, unlike standard-series and premium-series.<br>13. Plan capacity accordingly: one Business Critical vCore consumes four regional vCore units against your subscription quota, versus one for General Purpose.<br>10. Note the memory-to-vCore ratio holds only up to 64 vCores; above that, memory stays capped at 870.4 GB. | [1](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/purchasing-models)<br>[2](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/service-tiers-managed-instance-vcore)<br>[3](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) |
| 283 | `BC_G8IH (16 vCores)` | Managed instance | Business Critical | Premium-series memory optimized | 16 vCore / 217.6 GB | 2022-09 | medium | Generally available | [docs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/resource-limits) | [announcement](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/managed-instance/doc-changes-updates-release-notes-whats-new-archive) | 1. Purchasing model: vCore is the model Microsoft recommends. Choose it when you value flexibility, control, and transparency, want to scale compute and storage independently, or want to apply Azure Hybrid Benefit or reserved capacity pricing.<br>2. Use when the workload needs consistently low IO latency, about 1-2 ms, from locally attached SSD storage.<br>3. Use when reports, analytics, or read-only queries can be redirected to the free read-scale replica included in the tier.<br>4. Use when you need higher resiliency and faster recovery - one of three secondary replicas becomes the new primary immediately on failure.<br>5. Use when you need In-Memory OLTP, which neither General Purpose nor Next-gen General Purpose supports.<br>6. Use when advanced data corruption protection through automatic page repair matters.<br>7. Use when a failover group must guarantee an RPO of 5 seconds and an RTO of 30 seconds.<br>8. Configure 64 or more vCores if you need compute isolation - it is only supported at that size and above.<br>9. Plan capacity accordingly: one Business Critical vCore consumes four regional vCore units against your subscription quota, versus one for General Purpose.<br>10. Choose when the workload is memory-bound and needs 13.6 GB of memory per vCore, up to 870.4 GB per instance.<br>11. Choose when In-Memory OLTP needs the largest available allocation - memory optimized premium-series gives roughly double the premium-series limit.<br>12. Choose when Business Critical needs up to 16 TB of instance storage.<br>13. Check regional availability first - memory optimized premium-series is restricted to a subset of regions, unlike standard-series and premium-series.<br>14. ## Release milestones

| Milestone | Preview | GA | Confidence | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DTU purchasing model: Basic, Standard, Premium service tiers | 2014-04 | 2014-09 | medium | [link](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/new-azure-sql-database-service-tiers-generally-available-in-september-with-reduced-pricing-and-enhanced-sla/) |
| DTU Premium performance levels P4 and P11 | 2014-09 | 2015 | low | [link](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/new-azure-sql-database-service-tiers-generally-available-in-september-with-reduced-pricing-and-enhanced-sla/) |
| Expanded DTU performance levels S4, S6, S7, S9, S12 | 2016-02 | 2016 | low | [link](https://azure.microsoft.com/updates/azure-sql-database-new-performance-levels-and-storage-add-ons-in-public-preview/) |
| DTU Premium performance level P15 (4000 DTU) | 2016-02 | 2016-08 | medium | [link](https://azure.microsoft.com/updates?id=general-availability-new-azure-sql-database-premium-performance-level-p15) |
| vCore purchasing model (General Purpose / Business Critical, Gen4 + Gen5) | 2017-09 | 2018-04 | low | [link](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/a-flexible-new-way-to-purchase-azure-sql-database/) |
| Serverless compute tier (General Purpose, standard-series) | 2018-11 | 2019-11 | medium | [link](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/serverless-tier-overview) |
| Hyperscale service tier | 2018-10 | 2019-05 | medium | [link](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/announcing-azure-sql-database-hyperscale-public-preview/) |
| Serverless compute tier for Hyperscale | 2023-02 | 2024-02 | high | [link](https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azuresqlblog/general-availability-serverless-for-hyperscale-in-azure-sql-database/4053589) |
| DC-series hardware, 2-8 vCores (Intel SGX / Always Encrypted with secure enclaves) | 2019 | 2021-11 | low | [link](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/service-tiers-sql-database-vcore) |
| DC-series hardware, 10-40 vCores | 2023-07 | 2023-11 | high | [link](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-sql/database/doc-changes-updates-release-notes-whats-new-archive) |

### Milestone notes

- **DTU purchasing model: Basic, Standard, Premium service tiers** — Basic/Standard/Premium reached GA in September 2014; GA pricing took effect 2014-11-01.
- **DTU Premium performance levels P4 and P11** — P4 and P11 were introduced alongside the 2014 tier refresh; exact GA month not recoverable from a first-party dated announcement. Treat the year as approximate.
- **Expanded DTU performance levels S4, S6, S7, S9, S12** — Announced as public preview in the 2016 'new performance levels and storage add-ons' update; GA followed later in 2016. Exact GA month not confirmed.
- **DTU Premium performance level P15 (4000 DTU)** — Azure update: 'General availability: New Azure SQL Database Premium performance level, P15'.
- **vCore purchasing model (General Purpose / Business Critical, Gen4 + Gen5)** — Announced as a new purchasing option in September 2017 and generally available in the first half of 2018. The GA month is approximate.
- **DC-series hardware, 2-8 vCores (Intel SGX / Always Encrypted with secure enclaves)** — GA month reconstructed from the DC-series rollout; treat as approximate.
- **128 vCore compute size (General Purpose and Business Critical, standard-series)** — Learn what's-new archive, 2023: '128 vCore GA | June'. The original announcement blog post has been removed from Tech Community.
- **160 and 192 vCore options for Hyperscale premium-series** — Preview only as of the catalog date. Available for single databases and Hyperscale elastic pools.
- **Managed Instance memory optimized premium-series hardware (G8IH)** — The Learn what's-new archive places 'Memory optimized premium-series hardware GA' and '16 TB support in Business Critical GA' in 2022 without a month; contemporaneous coverage dates the announcement to 2022-09-28. The original blog post has been removed from Tech Community.
- **Instance pools (the only way to deploy a 2-vCore managed instance)** — A 2-vCore instance can only be deployed inside an instance pool, so 2-vCore SKUs became generally available when instance pools did.
- **Managed Instance Next-gen General Purpose service tier** — Billed as General Purpose. An architectural upgrade (Elastic SAN storage), not a separate ARM SKU name. The Learn what's-new archive dates the preview to March 2024 and GA to November 2025; the GA blog post went up on 2025-12-02.

## Retired and deprecated hardware families

Gen4 and M-series no longer appear in the Microsoft resource-limit tables at all, so their individual service-level objectives cannot be enumerated from a current Microsoft page — they are recorded at family level rather than invented as SKU rows. Fsv2-series is still fully documented and its sizes appear individually in the table above.

| Family | Status | Retired | Detail | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Gen4 hardware (General Purpose and Business Critical) | Retired | 2020-2023 | Cannot be provisioned, scaled up, or scaled down. Migrate to a supported hardware generation for wider vCore and storage scalability, accelerated networking, and better IO performance. | [link](https://azure.microsoft.com/updates/support-has-ended-for-gen-4-hardware-on-azure-sql-database/) |
| Fsv2-series hardware (General Purpose) | Retired | 2026-10-01 | Retired on 1 October 2026 and removed from the resource-limits article, so its 11 sizes (GP_Fsv2_8 to GP_Fsv2_72) can no longer be enumerated. Microsoft directs former users to Hyperscale premium-series or standard-series (Gen5). | [link](https://azure.microsoft.com/updates?id=485030) |
| M-series hardware (Business Critical) | Retired | 2023 | Removed from the Azure SQL Database hardware options. Microsoft positions Hyperscale premium-series memory optimized as the alternative, offering more memory at a lower price. | [link](https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azuresqlblog/announcing-ga-of-new-premium-series-hardware-options-for-azure-sql-database-hype/3679091) |
